\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\bigg)-\varepsilon(\tilde{w},\tilde{h},r)\bigg)\\
&\geq\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon(\tilde{w},\tilde{h},r)\Big)\frac{c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{8}}{2}\Big(\Big(\frac{c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{16}}{8}-\gamma\Big)-\varepsilon(\tilde{w},\tilde{h},r)\Big)\geq\frac{c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{24}}{128},\end{split}

by (
4.68
), (
4.54
) and (
4.57
), proving (
4.73
).

All we are left to prove now is (
4.74
).
For this, suppose that we are on the event
\mathcal{A}
and let
u^{\prime}
be the starting point of the crossing in
H^{S}_{\text{bottom}}
.
It is clear that the horizontal crossing of
B^{S}_{\text{bottom}}
from
x
has to cross the bouncing path in the last event defining
\mathcal{A}
.
Therefore, when
X^{u^{\prime}}
reaches the height
S-r
, it will either have crossed
B
horizontally (in which case we are done), or it will be to the right of
\pi_{1}(u)+w(h)/8
.
In this case, by the Lipschitz property of
X^{u^{\prime}}
, when this wall reaches height
S
, it will be to the right of
\pi_{1}(u)+w(h)/8-r
, which in turn is inside the box
B_{\text{top}}\cap\mathbb{R}\times[S,\infty)
.
This means that
X^{u^{\prime}}
will stay to the right of the horizontal crossing of
B_{\text{top}}
and therefore it will also cross
B
, proving (
4.74
), see Figure
8
.

This concludes the proof of (
4.65
) and finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.4
.

5
Bootstrapping fluctuations and proof of Theorem
1.2

The Russo-Seymour-Welsh estimates proved in the previous section allow us to obtain the following bootstrapping bound on the fluctuations.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume that
c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}\geq\frac{1}{4}
. For any fixed
\xi_{0}\in(0,1)
there exist a decay rate
c_{\ref{c:alpha}}
,
\xi\in(0,\xi_{0}]
and
c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}=c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}(c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}},\xi_{0})
such that, if

(5.1)

w(h_{0})\geq h_{0}^{\xi},

for some
h_{0}\geq c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}
, then

(5.2)

w(h)\geq c_{\ref{c:fluctuation}}h^{\xi},

for all
h\geq h_{0}
.

Proof.

To emphasize that there is no cyclic choice of constants inside the proof, we present their choice beforehand.
Although it should be noted that the exact values of these constants is not important and other values would make the proof go through as well. Start by fixing
c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}=\tfrac{1}{4}
and set

(5.3)

n:=\left\lceil\frac{\ln(8)}{-\ln(1-2^{-118})}\right\rceil\quad\text{and}\quad\xi:=\frac{\ln(129/128)}{\ln(4n)}\wedge\xi_{0}.

Choose now
\alpha>1+\xi^{-1}>2
. Finally, set

(5.4)

c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}=c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}(c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}},\xi_{0})=\left\lceil\left(80c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}n\right)^{1/(\alpha-2)}\right\rceil\vee\big(c_{\ref{c:h_large_rsw}}(\tfrac{1}{4},c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}},\alpha,\xi)+64\wedge c_{\ref{c:large_h}}(\tfrac{1}{4})\big),

where
c_{\ref{c:large_h}}
is such that (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) hold and
c_{\ref{c:h_large_rsw}}
is given by Lemma
4.8
.
By Proposition
4.4
, using that
c_{\ref{c:alpha}}>\xi^{-1}+1
and
h_{0}\geq c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}
, if
w(h_{0})\geq h_{0}^{\xi}
then

(5.5)

\inf_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),3h_{0}\big)\big)\geq\frac{c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{54}}{2^{10}}\geq 2^{-118}.

Now we use Lemma
3.1
with
h=3h_{0}
to obtain

(5.6)

\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),4nh_{0})^{c}\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),3h_{0})^{c}\Big)^{n}+n\varepsilon\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),3h_{0},h_{0}\big).

By Lemma
6.2
, we obtain, since
w(h_{0})\leq h_{0}
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),4nh_{0}\big)^{c}\Big)&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),3h_{0})^{c}\Big)^{n}\\
&\quad+c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}n\Big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0})3h_{0}+\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0})+3h_{0}\big)h_{0}+h_{0}^{2}\Big)h_{0}^{-c_{\ref{c:alpha}}}\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\tfrac{129}{128}w(h_{0}),3h_{0})^{c}\Big)^{n}+10c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}nh_{0}^{-c_{\ref{c:alpha}}+2}\\
&\leq\big(1-2^{-118}\big)^{n}+10c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}nh_{0}^{-c_{\ref{c:alpha}}+2}\\
&\overset{\text{(see below)}}{\leq}\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{8}<\frac{1}{2},\end{split}

where we used for the last line that
\alpha>2
and that, by (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle n\geq\frac{\ln(8)}{-\ln(1-2^{-118})}\text{ and }h_{0}\geq\big(80c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}n\big)^{1/(\alpha-2)}.

This implies that

(5.9)

\displaystyle w(4nh_{0})\geq\frac{129}{128}w(h_{0})\geq\frac{129}{128}h_{0}^{\xi}\geq\frac{129}{128(4n)^{\xi}}\left(4nh_{0}\right)^{\xi}\;\;\overset{\eqref{defn_xi}}{\geq}\;\;\left(4nh_{0}\right)^{\xi}.

Repeating inductively the steps from (
5.7
) to (
5.9
), we obtain

(5.10)

w\big((4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)>\big((4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{\xi},\quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{N}.

We now claim that

(5.11)

w(h)\geq\left(\frac{h}{4n}\right)^{\xi},\quad\text{for all }h\geq h_{0},

which concludes the proof with the choice
c_{\ref{c:fluctuation}}=(4n)^{-\xi}
.

In order to verify (
5.11
), note that for all
h\geq h_{0}
, there exists a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h\in[(4n)^{k}h_{0},(4n)^{k+1}h_{0})
and notice that

(5.12)

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\big(\tfrac{h}{4n}\big)^{\xi},h\big)^{c}\Big)&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\big(\tfrac{h}{4n}\big)^{\xi},(4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{c}\Big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\big(\tfrac{(4n)^{k+1}h_{0}}{4n}\big)^{\xi},(4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{c}\Big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big((4n)^{k\xi}h_{0}^{\xi},(4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{c}\Big).\end{split}

This in particular implies

\frac{1}{\Vol(\mathcal{L}_{1})}\int_{u\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\big(\tfrac{h}{4n}\big)^{\xi},h\big)^{c}\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\leq\frac{1}{\Vol(\mathcal{L}_{1})}\int_{u\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\mathbb{P}\Big(H^{u}\big(\big((4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{\xi},(4n)^{k}h_{0}\big)^{c}\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u<\frac{1}{2},

by (
5.10
), which concludes the proof.
∎

We are finally in position to establish our main result.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
and choose
\xi_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
. Let
c_{\ref{c:alpha}}>0
,
\xi\in(0,\xi_{0})
, and
c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}(c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}},\xi_{0})
be as in Lemma
5.1
.
Recall the transition probabilities (
1.1
).
To generate the steps of
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, we will consider a sequence
(U_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
and declare that
X_{n+1}-X_{n}=-1
if and only if
U_{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\delta\big(\textbf{1}\{f{(X_{n},n)}\geq 0\}-\textbf{1}\{f{(X_{n},n)}<0\}\big)
.
In particular, note that on the event

(5.13)

E_{h,\delta}=\bigcap_{n=0}^{h-1}\big\{U_{n}\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta]\cup(\tfrac{1}{2}+\delta,1]\big\},

we can couple
(X_{n};0\leq n\leq h)
with a simple random walk
(Y_{n};0\leq n\leq h)
. Hence, for
h\geq 1
, and any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

(5.14)

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\big(V^{u}(h^{\xi_{0}},h)\big)&\overset{\text{(see below)}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}(E_{h,\delta}^{c})+\mathbb{P}\Big(|Y_{h}|\leq 2h^{\xi_{0}}\Big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{h,\delta}^{c})+\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{Y_{h}}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right|\leq 2h^{-\varepsilon}\right)\leq 2h\delta+\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{Y_{h}}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right|\leq 2h^{-\varepsilon}\right),\end{split}

where in the first line above, we used the fact that, in order for the random walk to cross the box when reflected on its right face, it needs to be entirely contained in the box
u+[-h^{\xi_{0}},2h^{\xi_{0}}]\times[0,h]
. This follows from the reflection principle, using the random variables
1-U_{n}
to determine the random walk
Y
when it is to the right of the face
u+\{h^{\xi_{0}}\}\times[0,h]
.

Using the Central Limit Theorem, we can choose
h_{0}\geq c_{\ref{c:bootstrap0}}
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
), then choose
\delta>0
small enough (depending on
h_{0}
) such that the probability above is smaller than
1/2
and thus

(5.15)

w(h_{0})\geq h_{0}^{\xi_{0}}\geq h_{0}^{\xi}.

By Lemma
5.1
,

(5.16)

w(h)\geq c_{\ref{c:fluctuation}}h^{\xi},\quad\text{for all }h\geq h_{0},

This lower bound on
w(h)
can be understood as a lower bound on the fluctuations of
X_{n}
.

In order to get the exact statement (
1.6
) in Theorem
1.2
, we proceed with a geometric construction.
By possibly increasing the value of
h_{0}
, we can assume that
w(h)\geq 512
, for all
h\geq h_{0}
. Fix
j=3\cdot 128
and for
n\geq 3jh_{0}
write
h=\frac{n}{3j}
and let

\displaystyle B_{1}=[-w(h)-4,0]\times[0,n],\quad B_{2}=[-w(h)-4,2w(h)+4]\times[0,n],

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad B_{3}=[w(h),2w(h)+4]\times[0,n].

We can now use crossing events to estimate

(5.17)

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Big(X_{n}\geq\frac{c_{\ref{c:fluctuation}}}{(3j)^{\xi}}n^{\xi}\Big)&\overset{\eqref{e:w_lower}}{\geq}\mathbb{P}\big(X_{n}\geq w(h)\big)\geq\mathbb{P}\big(V(B_{1})\cap H(B_{2})\cap V(B_{3})\big)\\
&\overset{\eqref{e:fkg}}{\geq}\mathbb{P}\big(V(B_{1})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(H(B_{2})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(V(B_{3})\big)\\
&\overset{\text{Corol.~\ref{c:stretch_vertically}}}{\geq}\Big(\frac{c_{\ref{c:vertical_rsw}}c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{8}}{2}\Big)^{2\cdot 3j}\mathbb{P}\big(H(B_{2})\big)\overset{\text{Corol.~\ref{c:stretch_horizontally}}}{\geq}\Big(\frac{c_{\ref{c:vertical_rsw}}c_{\ref{c:ellipticity}}^{8}}{2}\Big)^{2\cdot 3j}c_{\ref{c:c_rsw}}^{j},\end{split}

finishing the proof of the theorem by possibly taking a smaller
\xi
.
∎

Remark 9
.

Let us now address the perturbative assumption
\delta<c_{\ref{c:perturbative}}
in our main result.
By looking at Lemma
5.1
it becomes clear that the reason why we need a small interaction between the random walk and the random environment is to obtain a lower bound on fluctuations, or more precisely, a lower bound on
w(h_{0})
.

This triggering assumption for the induction was necessary because in Lemma
4.8
we can only obtain a horizontal RSW if we have an a priori lower bound on
w(h)
at the previous scale, see (
4.50
).

Digging a bit deeper into Lemma
4.8
, we see that (
4.50
) was used to obtain a decoupling in (
4.53
).
Therefore, at the end of the day, the reason behind our perturbative assumption is to avoid having a very thin and tall box
B_{\text{top}}
.
This would mean that the search for a horizontal crossing of
B_{\text{top}}
could potentially generate a negative information in a large region, forcing us to position
B_{\text{bottom}}
too far down, which would break our argument.

Note that if a random environment satisfies Definition
1.1
with
\varepsilon(w,h,r)
that decays very fast with
h
(say exponentially), then we do not expect to need a perturbative assumption on
\delta
.
But it is an open question whether Proposition
4.4
holds without (
4.50
) in general.

6
Examples

6.1
Gaussian fields

Fix a function
q:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
not identically null, and that is invariant under vertical reflexions of the plane.
Assume further that there exists
\beta>2
such that

(6.1)

q(x)\leq c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}|x|^{-\beta},\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus{\{o\}},

and that
q(o)\leq c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}
.
Consider now a family
\big(W_{x}\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
of i.i.d. standard
\normal(0,1)
random variables and define the random Gaussian field
\big(g_{x}\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
via

(6.2)

g_{x}=\sum_{y}q(x-y)W_{y}.

Given the Gaussian field
g
, consider now the environment given by

(6.3)

f(x)=\sign(g_{x})=\textbf{1}_{\{g_{x}>0\}}-\textbf{1}_{\{g_{x}<0\}}.

Observe that
f
satisfies (
T
) and (
R
) by construction. It remains then to verify that it also satisfies (
FKG
) and Definition
1.1
for
\varepsilon
as in (
1.5
).

The (
FKG
) inequality follows from the following lemma.

Proposition 6.1

(FKG inequality for Gaussian fields
[
33
]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be two increasing events depending on finitely many coordinates of
g
. Then

(FKG)

\P\big(g\in A\cap B\big)\geq\P\big(g\in A\big)\P\big(g\in B\big).

The verification of Definition
1.1
with
\varepsilon
as in (
1.5
) requires a bit more of work and we state it as a lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

The field
f
satisfies the decoupling condition in Definition
1.1
, with decay rate given by

(6.4)

\varepsilon(w,h,r)=c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}\big(wh+(w+h)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}},

where
c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}
is a positive constant.

In order to prove the lemma, we start by bounding the covariance of the field
g
.

Observe that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
g_{x}
is a centered Gaussian random variable with variance

(6.5)

\sigma_{q}^{2}=\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}q(y)^{2}<\infty,

since we are assuming that
\beta>2
.
The covariance structure in this field is given by

(6.6)

\cov\big(g_{x},g_{y}\big)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}q(x-z)q(y-z)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}q(x-y-z)q(z)=q*q(x-y).

Due to (
6.1
), we have

(6.7)

\begin{split}q*q(x)&=\sum_{z:\,|z-x|\geq\frac{1}{2}|x|}q(x-z)q(z)+\sum_{z:\,|z-x|<\frac{1}{2}|x|}q(x-z)q(z)\\
&\leq 2\sum_{y:\,|y|\geq\frac{1}{2}|x|}c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}^{2}|y|^{-\beta}\leq c_{\ref{c:correlation_convolution}}|x|^{-\beta+2},\end{split}

for some constant
c_{\ref{c:correlation_convolution}}>0
.

We now construct finite-range approximations for
g
, by defining, for each
r>0
, the truncated filed
\big(g_{x}^{r}\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
via

(6.8)

g^{r}_{x}=\sum_{y:\,|x-y|\leq\frac{r}{2}}q(x-y)W_{y}.

Notice that, if
|x-y|>r
, then
g_{x}^{r}
and
g_{y}^{r}
are independent.

Proposition 6.3
.

There exists a positive constant
c_{\ref{c:approximation}}>0
such that, for every
r\geq 2
,
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
t\geq 0
,

(6.9)

\P\big(|g_{x}-g_{x}^{r}|\geq t\big)\leq 2e^{-c_{\ref{c:approximation}}t^{2}r^{2\beta-2}}.

Proof.

By translation invariance, it suffices to consider the case where
x=0
.
We now observe that the random variable

(6.10)

g_{0}-g_{0}^{r}=\sum_{y:\,|y|>\frac{r}{2}}q(-y)W_{y}

has the normal distribution with mean
0
and variance
\sum_{y:\,|y|>\frac{r}{2}}q(y)^{2}
.
Standard tail estimates for Gaussian variables now yield

(6.11)

\P\big(|g_{0}-g_{0}^{r}|\geq t\big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sum_{y:\,|y|>\frac{r}{2}}q(y)^{2}}}.

The proof is now complete by combining the estimate above with

(6.12)

\sum_{y:\,|y|>\frac{r}{2}}q(y)^{2}\leq c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}^{2}\sum_{y:\,|y|>\frac{r}{2}}|y|^{-2\beta}\leq 4c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}^{2}\sum_{n>\frac{r}{2}}n^{-2\beta+1}\leq\frac{2c_{\ref{c:correlation_decay}}^{2}}{\beta-1}\Big(\frac{r}{2}-1\Big)^{-2\beta+2},

finishing the proof of the proposition.
∎

Analogously to (
6.8
), we introduce the finite-range approximations of the environment by defining, for every
r\geq 1
,

(6.13)

f^{r}(x)=\textbf{1}_{\{g_{x}^{r}\geq 0\}}-\textbf{1}_{\{g_{x}^{r}<0\}}.

Finally, we provide a result for the environment
f
which is analogous to Proposition
6.3
.

Proposition 6.4
.

There exists
c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}>0
such that, for every
r\geq 2
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

(6.14)

\P\big(f(x)\neq f^{r}(x)\big)\leq c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}.

Proof.

Once again, it suffices to consider the case
x=0
by translation invariance. If
f(0)\neq f^{r}(0)
, then for every fixed
t\geq 0
, either
|g_{0}-g_{0}^{r}|\geq t
or
|g_{0}|\leq t

This yields

(6.15)

\P\big(f(0)\neq f^{r}(0)\big)\leq\P\big(|g_{0}-g_{0}^{r}|\geq t\big)+\P\big(|g_{0}|\leq t\big).

The first term in the right-hand side of the equation above is bounded in Proposition
6.3
. As for the second term, notice that
g_{0}\sim\normal(0,\sigma_{q})
, with
\sigma_{q}
given by (
6.5
), and then bound the density of
g_{0}
by
(2\pi\sigma_{q})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
to obtain

(6.16)

\P\big(|g_{0}|\leq t\big)\leq t\sigma_{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

We now choose
t=r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}
to conclude the proof.
∎

Proof.

The statement follows immediately by combining Proposition
6.4
and union bounds. Since
f^{r}
is constructed with
g^{r}
as in (
6.13
), it is an
r
-dependent field.
∎

We are can now prove that the field
f
satisfies the decoupling condition.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Recall the definition of
f^{r}
in (
6.13
). Fix a rectangle
C=[a,a+w]\times[b,b+h]\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and define the field

(6.17)

{f}^{C,r}(x)=\begin{cases}f^{r}(x),&\text{ if }\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)\leq r;\\
f^{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)}(x),&\text{ if }\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)>r.\end{cases}

We first verify that the field defined above indeed satisfies the first statement of the lemma. Let
A\subset C
and
B\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(A,B)\geq r
. Notice that
{f}^{C,r}\big|_{A}=f^{r}\big|_{A}
and thus this restriction is determined by the random variables
\big(W_{y}\big)_{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(y,A)\leq\frac{r}{2}}
. Let us now study
{f}^{C,r}\big|_{B}
: if
x\in B
is such that
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)\leq r
, then
{f}^{C,r}(x)
is determined by
\big(W_{y}\big)_{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(y,x)\leq\frac{r}{2}}
. On the other hand, if
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)>r
, then
{f}^{C,r}(x)
is determined by
\big(W_{y}\big)_{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(y,x)\leq\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)}{2}}
. Therefore, regardless of the choice of
B
,
{f}^{C,r}\big|_{A}
and
{f}^{C,r}\big|_{B}
are determined by disjoint collections of the family of Gaussian variables
\big(W_{x}\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
and thus are independent.

Finally, let us bound the probability that
f
and
{f}^{C,r}
are different. Fix
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and notice that, if
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)\leq r
, Proposition
6.4
yields

(6.18)

\P\big(f(x)\neq{f}^{C,r}(x)\big)\leq c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}.

If, on the other hand,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)>r
, then

(6.19)

\P\big(f(x)\neq{f}^{C,r}(x)\big)=\P\big(f(x)\neq f^{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)}(x)\big)\leq c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}.

This yields

(6.20)

\begin{split}\P\big(f\neq{f}^{C,r}\big)&\leq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\P\big(f(x)\neq{f}^{C,r}(x)\big)\\
&\leq 4c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\big(|C|+\Per(C)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}+\sum_{x:\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)>r}\P\big(f(x)\neq{f}^{C,r}(x)\big)\\
&\leq 4c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\big(|C|+\Per(C)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}+\sum_{x:\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)>r}c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,C)^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}\\
&\leq 4c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\big(|C|+\Per(C)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}+\sum_{k>r}8c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}\big(k+\Per(C)\big)k^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}}\\
&\leq c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}\big(wh+(w+h)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\beta+\frac{3}{2}},\end{split}

concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 10
.

The Gaussian free field also satisfies (
T
) and (
R
) by construction.
The fact that it satisfies (
FKG
) also follows from
[
33
]
, since this field is positively correlated.
An analogous to Proposition
6.3
above for the
d
-dimensional discrete Gaussian Free Field
\varphi
can be found in
[
20
, Lemma 3.2]
.
More precisely, there exist constants
c,C>0
and, for every
L>0
,
L
-dependent approximations
\varphi^{L}
that satisfy, for all
t>0
,

(6.21)

\P\big(|\varphi_{x}-\varphi^{L}_{x}|\geq t\big)\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}L^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}.

If one defines
f
as in (
6.13
) but for the discrete Gaussian Free Field
\varphi
and its finite-range approximations
\varphi^{L}
in place of
g^{r}
, the proof of Lemma
6.2
above can be combined with the above estimate to obtain the bound

(6.22)

\P\big(f(x)\neq f^{L}(x)\big)\leq c_{\ref{c:approximation_environment}}L^{-\frac{d-3}{4}},

for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and every
L\geq 1
.
This implies that the Gaussian Free Field in dimension
d
satisfies the Definition
1.1
with decay rate

(6.23)

\varepsilon(w,h,L)=c_{\ref{c:bad_coupling}}\big(wh+(w+h)L+L^{2}\big)L^{-\frac{d-3}{4}}.

6.2
Confetti

Let us now turn our attention to the confetti random environment described informally in Section
1
.
We start by providing a rigorous definition for it.

Consider a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,1]
with intensity measure
\mu=\lambda(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u)\otimes\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}(\delta_{-1}+\delta_{1})\otimes\lambda(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w)
, where
\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure and
\nu
is a fixed reference distribution.
Recall from (
1.11
) we are assuming that
\nu
has tails that decay polynomially for some exponent
\alpha>2
.
We denote
\P
the probability law of the Poisson point process defined on an appropriate sample space
\Omega
whose elements are point-measures

(6.24)

\omega=\sum_{i\geq 0}\delta_{(u_{i},r_{i},d_{i},w_{i})}.

This space is endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
generated by the evaluation maps
g_{A}(\omega)=\omega(A)
, for every Borel measurable set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,1]
.

Each point in the realization of that Poisson point process has the form
(u_{i},r_{i},d_{i},w_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,1]
, and is associated with a colored ball in the plane, where
u_{i}
and
r_{i}
determine respectively its center and radius, while
d_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
determines the color assigned to it, where
1
is interpreted as the color red and
-1
as the color blue. The variable
w_{i}
will be used as a tie breaking rule.
Observe that, conditioned on the collection of centers
(u_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, the random variables
(r_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
(d_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, and
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
are independent and act as decorations of the planar Poisson point process.
The variable
d_{i}
assumes the values
1
or
-1
with equal probability.

Recalling the representation
\omega=\sum_{i\geq 0}\delta_{(u_{i},r_{i},d_{i},w_{i})}
, we say that

(6.25)

\begin{array}[]{c}\parbox[c]{345.0pt}{\centering\emph{the ball $i$ covers a point $z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$} if $\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(z,u_{i})\leq r_{i}$.\@add@centering}\end{array}

Informally, every point
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
will be assigned the same color as a ball uniformly chosen among all balls that cover it.
In case a certain point in the plane is not covered by any ball, it will be assigned the color
0
(which may me interpreted as the color gray).
In order to construct this coloring precisely, we define the index of the largest weight of a ball covering a point
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as

(6.26)

I_{z}=\underset{i}{\argmax}\big\{w_{i};\,z\text{ is covered by }i\big\},

where we implicitly assume that
I_{z}=\infty
if no ball covers the point
z
.

Given a realization
\omega
, we now define the environment
f^{\omega}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\{-1,0,1\}
via

(6.27)

f^{\omega}(z)=\begin{cases}d_{I_{z}}\qquad&\text{if $I_{z}<\infty$ and}\\
0,\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We reader may consult Figure
2
for an illustration of the random environment we just constructed.

Remark 11
.

Note that the variables
\{I_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}
act solely in order to define the color of a point covered by many boxes balls.
Any other unambiguous tie-breaking strategy would also suffice for our results to hold.

Once again (
T
) and (
R
) follows directly from the construction of the model, since the Poisson point process considered satisfies these two properties.

Let us now verify that
f
satisfies the (
FKG
) inequality.
It will be convenient for us to decompose the Poisson point process
\omega
in (
6.24
) into two parts
\omega=\omega_{+}+\omega_{-}
where

(6.28)

\omega_{\pm}=\sum_{i\geq 0,d_{i}=\pm}\delta_{(u_{i},r_{i},d_{i},w_{i})}.

Each of the
\omega_{\pm}
is a Poisson point processes with intensity
\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda(\text{d}u)\otimes\nu(\text{d}r)\otimes(\delta_{-1}+\delta_{1})\otimes\lambda(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w)
(notice that the variable
d_{i}
is fixed).

We say an event
A
is increasing if, for any
(\omega_{+},\omega_{-}),(\tilde{\omega}_{+},\tilde{\omega}_{-})\in\Omega
such that
\omega_{+}\leq\tilde{\omega}_{+}
and
\omega_{-}\geq\tilde{\omega}_{-}
,

(6.29)

(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})\in A\text{ implies }(\tilde{\omega}_{+},\tilde{\omega}_{-})\in A.

Remark 12
.

Notice that, for any coloring
f
, we can write
f=f(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})
, where
f
is an increasing function of
\omega_{+}
and decreasing on the variable
\omega_{-}
. In particular, an event
A
depending on the coloring
f
is monotone increasing in the sense of (
1.2
) if
f\leq\tilde{f}
and
f\in A
implies
\tilde{f}\in A
.

The next proposition extends the FKG inequality for our setting, implying that increasing events are positively correlated.

Proposition 6.5
.

For any two increasing events
A
and
B
,

(6.30)

\P(A\cap B)\geq\P(A)\P(B).

Proof.

Fix two increasing events
A
and
B
. We start by applying the FKG inequality for Poisson point processes (in the variable
\omega_{-}
) in the conditional setting. This gives us, for each configuration
\omega_{+}
fixed,

(6.31)

\E\big(\textbf{1}_{A}(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})\textbf{1}_{B}(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})\big|\omega_{+}\big)\geq\E\big(\textbf{1}_{A}(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})\big|\omega_{+}\big)\E\big(\textbf{1}_{B}(\omega_{+},\omega_{-})\big|\omega_{+}\big).

We further notice that the conditional expectations
\omega_{+}\mapsto\E\big(\textbf{1}_{A}\big|\omega_{+}\big)
and
\omega_{+}\mapsto\E\big(\textbf{1}_{B}\big|\omega_{+}\big)
are both monotone increasing, since
A
and
B
are monotone in each of the variables separately. Another application of the the usual FKG inequality yields

(6.32)

\begin{split}\P(A\cap B)&\geq\E\big(\P(A|\omega_{+})\P(B|\omega_{+})\big)\geq\E\big(\P(A|\omega_{+})\big)\E\big(\P(B|\omega_{+})\big)=\P(A)\P(B),\end{split}

concluding the proof.
∎

The next lemma proves that the confetti random environment satisfies a decoupling condition stated in Definition
1.1
with
\varepsilon
as in (
1.5
).

Lemma 6.6
.

The Confetti coloring
f
satisfies the decoupling condition from Definition
1.1
with decay rate given by

(6.33)

\varepsilon(w,h,r)=c_{\ref{c:boolean_decoupling}}\big(wh+(w+h)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha},

where
c_{\ref{c:boolean_decoupling}}>0
depends only on
c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}
.

Note the similarity between the above and Lemma
6.2
.
Before proceeding to the proof of the above lemma, we state a preliminary result.
Fixed a rectangle
R=[a,b]\times[c,d]
and
r>0
, we consider the set
of triples
(u,s,d)
, corresponding to balls with radius at least
r
that intersect the box
R
:

(6.34)

\mathcal{B}(R,r)=\Big\{(u,s,d,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,1];s\geq r\text{ and }B(u,s)\cap R\neq\emptyset\Big\}.

The next lemma bounds the probability that a point in the Poisson process falls into
\mathcal{B}(R,r)
.

Lemma 6.7
.

There exists
c_{\ref{c:large_balls}}>0
such that, for any
R=[a,b]\times[c,d]
and
r\geq 1
,

(6.35)

\P\Big(\omega\big(\mathcal{B}(R,r)\big)>0\Big)\leq c_{\ref{c:large_balls}}\big(\Vol(R)+r\Per(R)+r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha},

where
\omega
denotes the Poisson point process (
6.24
),
\Vol(R)
and
\Per(R)
denote respectively the volume and perimeter of
R
.

Proof.

Markov’s inequality immediately implies

(6.36)

\P\Big(\omega\big(\mathcal{B}(R,r)\big)>0\Big)\leq\E\Big(\omega\big(\mathcal{B}(R,r)\big)\Big),

so that it suffices to bound the above expectation.
Now we have:

(6.37)

\begin{split}\E\Big(\omega(&\mathcal{B}(R,r))\Big)=\int\nu\Big(\big[\max\big\{r,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(u,R)\big\},\infty\big)\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda(u)\\
&\overset{\eqref{e:nu_decay}}{\leq}\int c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}\max\big\{r,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(u,R)\big\}^{-\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda(u)\\
&=\int_{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(u,R)\leq r}c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}r^{-\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda(u)+\int_{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(u,R)>r}c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(u,R)^{-\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda(u)\\
&\leq c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}\big(\Vol(R)+r\Per(R)+4r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha}+\int_{r}^{\infty}c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}\big(\Per(R)+2\pi s\big)s^{-\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\\
&=c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}\big(\Vol(R)+r\Per(R)+4r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha}+\frac{c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}}{\alpha-1}\Per(R)r^{-\alpha+1}+\frac{c_{\ref{c:decay_nu}}}{\alpha-2}\;2\pi\;r^{-\alpha+2}\\
&\leq c_{\ref{c:large_balls}}\big(\Vol(R)+r\Per(R)+r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha},\end{split}

which concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to provide the proof of our decoupling inequality.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Given a rectangle
D=[a,a+w]\times[b,b+h]
and a radius
r>0
, we define the Poisson Point process
\omega^{D,r}
through

(6.38)

\omega^{D,r}:={\bf 1}_{\mathcal{B}(D,r)^{c}}\cdot\omega.

This new process is essentially obtained by discarding every ball that touches
D
, but has radius larger or equal to
r
.
This allows us to introduce the desired field

(6.39)

f^{D,r}:=f^{\omega^{D,r}}

and all we are left to do is to prove that the two conditions (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) hold.

The finite-range condition (
1.4
) is a consequence of the fact that all balls intersecting
D
in
\omega^{D,r}
have radius at most
r
.
While the coupling condition (
1.3
) is implied by Lemma
6.7
and the following calculation

(6.40)

\begin{split}\P\big(f^{C,r}\neq f\big)\quad&=\quad\P\Big(\omega\big(\mathcal{B}(D,r)>0\big)\Big)\\
&\overset{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle{\text{Lemma}~\ref{l:large_ball_intersection}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle{\text{Lemma}~\ref{l:large_ball_intersection}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{\text{Lemma}~\ref{l:large_ball_intersection}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Lemma}~\ref{l:large_ball_intersection}}$\hss}}}{\leq}\quad c_{\ref{c:large_balls}}\big(\Vol(D)+\Per(D)r+r^{2}\big)r^{-\alpha}\leq\varepsilon(w,h,r),\end{split}

recalling that
h,w\geq 1
and properly choosing
c_{\ref{c:boolean_decoupling}}
.
∎
